# 12. Pipeline permissions

> Corporate/L3 deep-dive training guide for Red Hat OpenShift AI user and access management.

- **Primary objective:** Authorize pipeline server administration, pipeline authoring, execution, artifact access, and service-account operations per project.
- **Audience:** Senior OpenShift administrators, OpenShift AI platform engineers, MLOps engineers, security architects, IAM teams, SREs, and auditors.
- **Documentation baseline:** Red Hat OpenShift AI Self-Managed 3.5 and OpenShift Container Platform 4.21.
- **Operating rule:** Validate supported APIs, feature status, and exact resource names on the installed cluster before production rollout.
- **Depth:** Architecture, implementation from scratch, production patterns, YAML, CLI, validation, audit, troubleshooting, and hands-on labs.

---

## 1. Executive summary

Pipeline permissions is a control domain rather than a single console setting. Authorize pipeline server administration, pipeline authoring, execution, artifact access, and service-account operations per project.

A production design must separate authentication, platform admission, namespace authorization, resource-level authorization, workload identity, data access, network access, and evidence collection. A successful login proves identity; it does not automatically prove authorization to a project, workbench, pipeline, model endpoint, registry, secret, or storage bucket.

OpenShift AI consumes OpenShift authentication and authorization. The dashboard can restrict platform entry through configured administrator and allowed groups, while Kubernetes and OpenShift RBAC determine what an identity can do inside namespaces and against cluster-scoped resources.

### 1.1 Naming clarification

The names `rhoai-admins` and `rhoai-users` in this training pack are enterprise-selected group names. They are not assumed to be immutable product-reserved defaults. Red Hat documentation frequently uses example names such as `rhods-admins` and `rhods-users`; current OpenShift AI releases allow existing OpenShift groups to be selected for administrator and user access.

### 1.2 Senior-engineer mental model

Use the following decision chain for every access request:

1. Who is the subject: human, group, service account, workload, or external client?
2. How is the subject authenticated and how is identity mapped?
3. Is the subject allowed to enter the OpenShift AI dashboard?
4. Which namespace or project contains the target resource?
5. Which API group, resource, subresource, and verb are required?
6. Which data plane credentials or network paths are additionally required?
7. What expiry, ownership, review, and evidence requirements apply?
8. How will access be revoked without leaving active sessions, tokens, PVCs, or secrets behind?

### 1.3 Topic-specific scope

- **Actor 1:** pipeline administrator
- **Actor 2:** pipeline author
- **Actor 3:** pipeline runner
- **Actor 4:** MLOps engineer
- **Actor 5:** auditor
- **Resource 1:** `DataSciencePipelinesApplication`
- **Resource 2:** `PipelineRun`
- **Resource 3:** `Workflow`
- **Resource 4:** `ServiceAccount`
- **Resource 5:** `Secret`
- **Resource 6:** `Route`
- **Resource 7:** `PVC`
- **Resource 8:** `object storage`
- **Primary risk 1:** runner service account over-privilege
- **Primary risk 2:** artifact bucket exposure
- **Primary risk 3:** pipeline code tampering
- **Primary risk 4:** secret injection
- **Primary risk 5:** cross-project execution

## 2. Architecture and control layers

### 2.1 Identity source

LDAP, Active Directory, HTPasswd, OIDC, GitHub, or another supported provider establishes credentials and attributes.

**Relevance to Pipeline permissions:** The identity source layer must be designed so that authorize pipeline server administration, pipeline authoring, execution, artifact access, and service-account operations per project.

**Design checks:**
- Check 1: Verify how `DataSciencePipelinesApplication` is protected against runner service account over-privilege.
- Check 2: Verify how `PipelineRun` is protected against artifact bucket exposure.
- Check 3: Verify how `Workflow` is protected against pipeline code tampering.
- Check 4: Verify how `ServiceAccount` is protected against secret injection.
- Check 5: Verify how `Secret` is protected against cross-project execution.
- Check 6: Verify how `Route` is protected against runner service account over-privilege.

### 2.2 OpenShift OAuth

The cluster OAuth server validates the identity-provider response, maps identities to users, and issues access tokens.

**Relevance to Pipeline permissions:** The openshift oauth layer must be designed so that authorize pipeline server administration, pipeline authoring, execution, artifact access, and service-account operations per project.

**Design checks:**
- Check 1: Verify how `PipelineRun` is protected against artifact bucket exposure.
- Check 2: Verify how `Workflow` is protected against pipeline code tampering.
- Check 3: Verify how `ServiceAccount` is protected against secret injection.
- Check 4: Verify how `Secret` is protected against cross-project execution.
- Check 5: Verify how `Route` is protected against runner service account over-privilege.
- Check 6: Verify how `PVC` is protected against artifact bucket exposure.

### 2.3 OpenShift groups

Groups aggregate human users and are the preferred unit for platform and project role assignment.

**Relevance to Pipeline permissions:** The openshift groups layer must be designed so that authorize pipeline server administration, pipeline authoring, execution, artifact access, and service-account operations per project.

**Design checks:**
- Check 1: Verify how `Workflow` is protected against pipeline code tampering.
- Check 2: Verify how `ServiceAccount` is protected against secret injection.
- Check 3: Verify how `Secret` is protected against cross-project execution.
- Check 4: Verify how `Route` is protected against runner service account over-privilege.
- Check 5: Verify how `PVC` is protected against artifact bucket exposure.
- Check 6: Verify how `object storage` is protected against pipeline code tampering.

### 2.4 OpenShift AI platform admission

Configured administrator groups and allowed groups determine dashboard-level user type and entry.

**Relevance to Pipeline permissions:** The openshift ai platform admission layer must be designed so that authorize pipeline server administration, pipeline authoring, execution, artifact access, and service-account operations per project.

**Design checks:**
- Check 1: Verify how `ServiceAccount` is protected against secret injection.
- Check 2: Verify how `Secret` is protected against cross-project execution.
- Check 3: Verify how `Route` is protected against runner service account over-privilege.
- Check 4: Verify how `PVC` is protected against artifact bucket exposure.
- Check 5: Verify how `object storage` is protected against pipeline code tampering.
- Check 6: Verify how `DataSciencePipelinesApplication` is protected against secret injection.

### 2.5 Project authorization

Role and RoleBinding objects define namespace-scoped permissions.

**Relevance to Pipeline permissions:** The project authorization layer must be designed so that authorize pipeline server administration, pipeline authoring, execution, artifact access, and service-account operations per project.

**Design checks:**
- Check 1: Verify how `Secret` is protected against cross-project execution.
- Check 2: Verify how `Route` is protected against runner service account over-privilege.
- Check 3: Verify how `PVC` is protected against artifact bucket exposure.
- Check 4: Verify how `object storage` is protected against pipeline code tampering.
- Check 5: Verify how `DataSciencePipelinesApplication` is protected against secret injection.
- Check 6: Verify how `PipelineRun` is protected against cross-project execution.

### 2.6 Cluster authorization

ClusterRole and ClusterRoleBinding objects define reusable or cluster-wide privileges and require stronger governance.

**Relevance to Pipeline permissions:** The cluster authorization layer must be designed so that authorize pipeline server administration, pipeline authoring, execution, artifact access, and service-account operations per project.

**Design checks:**
- Check 1: Verify how `Route` is protected against runner service account over-privilege.
- Check 2: Verify how `PVC` is protected against artifact bucket exposure.
- Check 3: Verify how `object storage` is protected against pipeline code tampering.

### 2.7 Workload identity

Service accounts identify pods, jobs, pipelines, model servers, and automation.

**Relevance to Pipeline permissions:** The workload identity layer must be designed so that authorize pipeline server administration, pipeline authoring, execution, artifact access, and service-account operations per project.

**Design checks:**

### 2.8 Resource authorization

API groups, resources, subresources, resourceNames, and verbs create fine-grained control.

**Relevance to Pipeline permissions:** The resource authorization layer must be designed so that authorize pipeline server administration, pipeline authoring, execution, artifact access, and service-account operations per project.

**Design checks:**

S3, database, registry, object storage, and PVC access may require credentials beyond Kubernetes RBAC.

**Relevance to Pipeline permissions:** The data authorization layer must be designed so that authorize pipeline server administration, pipeline authoring, execution, artifact access, and service-account operations per project.

**Design checks:**

### 2.10 Network authorization

NetworkPolicy, routes, ingress, service mesh, and external firewalls control reachability.

**Relevance to Pipeline permissions:** The network authorization layer must be designed so that authorize pipeline server administration, pipeline authoring, execution, artifact access, and service-account operations per project.

**Design checks:**

- Check 3: Verify how `ServiceAccount` is protected against artifact bucket exposure.
- Check 4: Verify how `Secret` is protected against pipeline code tampering.

- Check 6: Verify how `PVC` is protected against cross-project execution.

### 2.11 Admission and runtime policy

SCC, Pod Security Admission, image policy, OPA, or Kyverno constrain what authorized users may create.

**Relevance to Pipeline permissions:** The admission and runtime policy layer must be designed so that authorize pipeline server administration, pipeline authoring, execution, artifact access, and service-account operations per project.

**Design checks:**
- Check 1: Verify how `Workflow` is protected against runner service account over-privilege.
- Check 2: Verify how `ServiceAccount` is protected against artifact bucket exposure.
- Check 3: Verify how `Secret` is protected against pipeline code tampering.
- Check 4: Verify how `Route` is protected against secret injection.
- Check 5: Verify how `PVC` is protected against cross-project execution.
- Check 6: Verify how `object storage` is protected against runner service account over-privilege.

### 2.12 Audit and governance

API audit logs, change tickets, Git history, approval records, and periodic certifications establish accountability.

**Relevance to Pipeline permissions:** The audit and governance layer must be designed so that authorize pipeline server administration, pipeline authoring, execution, artifact access, and service-account operations per project.

**Design checks:**

## 3. Reference architecture

The following logical flow is recommended for an enterprise deployment:

```text
[Enterprise IdP / AD / LDAP]
            | authentication + group source
            v
[OpenShift OAuth] ---> [User + Identity + OAuth token]
            |
            +--> [OpenShift Group objects]
            |          |
            |          +--> [OpenShift AI adminGroups / allowedGroups]
            |          +--> [Project RoleBindings]
            |          +--> [Model registry permissions]
            |
            +--> [Service accounts for pipelines/workbenches/model serving]
                       |
                       +--> [Namespaced Roles and scoped secrets]
                       +--> [Bound tokens and external workload identity]
```

### 3.1 Trust boundaries

#### Boundary 1: identity provider to OpenShift OAuth

- Authenticate both sides where possible and encrypt the channel.
- Authorize only the operations needed for Pipeline permissions.
- Log the request, decision, subject, target, and outcome.
- Test failure behavior when credentials expire or group membership changes.
- Define an owner and a rollback path for this boundary.

#### Boundary 2: OAuth server to API server

- Authenticate both sides where possible and encrypt the channel.
- Authorize only the operations needed for Pipeline permissions.
- Log the request, decision, subject, target, and outcome.
- Test failure behavior when credentials expire or group membership changes.
- Define an owner and a rollback path for this boundary.

#### Boundary 3: dashboard to OpenShift API

- Authenticate both sides where possible and encrypt the channel.
- Authorize only the operations needed for Pipeline permissions.
- Log the request, decision, subject, target, and outcome.
- Test failure behavior when credentials expire or group membership changes.
- Define an owner and a rollback path for this boundary.

#### Boundary 4: project namespace to another namespace

- Authenticate both sides where possible and encrypt the channel.
- Authorize only the operations needed for Pipeline permissions.
- Log the request, decision, subject, target, and outcome.
- Test failure behavior when credentials expire or group membership changes.
- Define an owner and a rollback path for this boundary.

#### Boundary 5: pod to API server

- Authenticate both sides where possible and encrypt the channel.
- Authorize only the operations needed for Pipeline permissions.
- Log the request, decision, subject, target, and outcome.
- Test failure behavior when credentials expire or group membership changes.
- Define an owner and a rollback path for this boundary.

#### Boundary 6: pod to object storage

- Authenticate both sides where possible and encrypt the channel.
- Authorize only the operations needed for Pipeline permissions.
- Log the request, decision, subject, target, and outcome.
- Test failure behavior when credentials expire or group membership changes.
- Define an owner and a rollback path for this boundary.

#### Boundary 7: external client to model endpoint

- Authenticate both sides where possible and encrypt the channel.
- Authorize only the operations needed for Pipeline permissions.
- Log the request, decision, subject, target, and outcome.
- Test failure behavior when credentials expire or group membership changes.
- Define an owner and a rollback path for this boundary.

#### Boundary 8: administrator workstation to cluster API

- Authenticate both sides where possible and encrypt the channel.
- Authorize only the operations needed for Pipeline permissions.
- Log the request, decision, subject, target, and outcome.
- Test failure behavior when credentials expire or group membership changes.
- Define an owner and a rollback path for this boundary.

## 4. Implementation from scratch

This implementation sequence is deliberately conservative. Execute it in a non-production cluster, capture evidence, and promote the configuration through GitOps.

### 4.1 Phase: Discover current state

#### Step 1: Discover current state control 1

- **Goal:** Establish a verifiable discover current state control for `DataSciencePipelinesApplication`.
- **Owner:** pipeline administrator.
- **Action:** Inspect or configure `DataSciencePipelinesApplication` and record the intended subject, scope, verbs, expiry, and approver.
- **CLI starting point:** `oc whoami`
- **Expected result:** Required access succeeds and access outside the approved scope fails.
- **Risk addressed:** runner service account over-privilege.
- **Evidence:** Store command output and declarative configuration in the access change record.
- **Rollback:** Remove the new binding or restore the previous Git revision, then retest active sessions and tokens.
- **Production note:** Never use successful dashboard login as the only authorization test.

#### Step 2: Discover current state control 2

- **Goal:** Establish a verifiable discover current state control for `PipelineRun`.
- **Owner:** pipeline author.
- **Action:** Inspect or configure `PipelineRun` and record the intended subject, scope, verbs, expiry, and approver.
- **CLI starting point:** `oc auth can-i --list`
- **Expected result:** Required access succeeds and access outside the approved scope fails.
- **Risk addressed:** artifact bucket exposure.
- **Evidence:** Store command output and declarative configuration in the access change record.
- **Rollback:** Remove the new binding or restore the previous Git revision, then retest active sessions and tokens.
- **Production note:** Never use successful dashboard login as the only authorization test.

## 5. Core manifests and command library

```yaml
apiVersion: user.openshift.io/v1
kind: Group
metadata:
  name: rhoai-users
  labels:
    access.platform.example.com/domain: rhoai
    access.platform.example.com/managed-by: identity-sync
users:
  - data-scientist-example
```

```yaml
apiVersion: user.openshift.io/v1
kind: Group
metadata:
  name: rhoai-admins
  labels:
    access.platform.example.com/domain: rhoai
    access.platform.example.com/privilege-tier: platform-admin
users:
  - rhoai-admin-example
```

```yaml
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: Role
metadata:
  name: pipeline-permissions-operator
  namespace: ds-team-a-dev
  labels:
    opendatahub.io/dashboard: "true"
    access.platform.example.com/topic: pipeline-permissions
rules:
  - apiGroups: [""]
    resources: ["pods", "services", "configmaps"]
    verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]
  - apiGroups: [""]
    resources: ["pods/log"]
    verbs: ["get"]
```

```yaml
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: RoleBinding
metadata:
  name: pipeline-permissions-team-binding
  namespace: ds-team-a-dev
subjects:
  - kind: Group
    apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io
    name: rhoai-team-a-pipeline-permissions
roleRef:
  apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io
  kind: Role
  name: pipeline-permissions-operator
```

```yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata:
  name: pipeline-permissions-automation
  namespace: ds-team-a-dev
  labels:
    access.platform.example.com/owner: team-a
    access.platform.example.com/purpose: pipeline-permissions
automountServiceAccountToken: false
```

```yaml
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: default-deny-pipeline-permissions
  namespace: ds-team-a-dev
spec:
  podSelector: {}
  policyTypes:
    - Ingress
    - Egress
```

```yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: ResourceQuota
metadata:
  name: pipeline-permissions-quota
  namespace: ds-team-a-dev
spec:
  hard:
    requests.cpu: "16"
    requests.memory: 64Gi
    limits.cpu: "32"
    limits.memory: 128Gi
    requests.storage: 1Ti
    count/secrets: "100"
```

```bash
# Inspect effective access
oc auth can-i --as=data-scientist-example --as-group=rhoai-users get pods -n ds-team-a-dev
oc auth can-i --as=data-scientist-example --as-group=rhoai-users get secrets -n ds-team-a-dev
oc auth can-i --as=system:serviceaccount:ds-team-a-dev:pipeline-permissions-automation create jobs.batch -n ds-team-a-dev
oc adm policy who-can delete pods -n ds-team-a-dev
oc get rolebindings -n ds-team-a-dev -o yaml
```

```yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata:
  name: pipeline-runner
  namespace: ds-team-a-dev
automountServiceAccountToken: false
---
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: Role
metadata:
  name: pipeline-runner
  namespace: ds-team-a-dev
rules:
  - apiGroups: ["batch"]
    resources: ["jobs"]
    verbs: ["get", "list", "watch", "create", "delete"]
  - apiGroups: [""]
    resources: ["pods", "pods/log", "configmaps"]
    verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]
```

---

## 6. Production control catalog

Each control below is written as an implementable review item. Tailor the exact API resources and verbs to the installed OpenShift AI release.

### Control 001: Identity control for PipelineRun

- **Intent:** Ensure pipeline administrator receives only the permissions required for Pipeline permissions in sandbox.
- **Implementation:** Bind an approved group or dedicated service account to the smallest suitable Role; avoid direct user bindings unless formally excepted.
- **Restriction:** Do not grant wildcard API groups, wildcard resources, or wildcard verbs without documented technical justification.
- **Validation:** Run `oc auth can-i` positive and negative tests for `PipelineRun` and review `oc adm policy who-can` output.
- **Failure prevented:** pipeline code tampering.
- **Evidence:** Git pull request.
- **Review frequency:** Quarterly for standard access; monthly or event-driven for privileged production access.
- **Revocation:** Remove the binding or group membership, terminate active sessions where applicable, and retest cached credentials.

### Control 002: Group Membership control for ServiceAccount

- **Intent:** Ensure pipeline author receives only the permissions required for Pipeline permissions in development.
- **Implementation:** Bind an approved group or dedicated service account to the smallest suitable Role; avoid direct user bindings unless formally excepted.
- **Restriction:** Do not grant wildcard API groups, wildcard resources, or wildcard verbs without documented technical justification.
- **Validation:** Run `oc auth can-i` positive and negative tests for `ServiceAccount` and review `oc adm policy who-can` output.
- **Failure prevented:** runner service account over-privilege.
- **Evidence:** approved ticket.
- **Review frequency:** Quarterly for standard access; monthly or event-driven for privileged production access.
- **Revocation:** Remove the binding or group membership, terminate active sessions where applicable, and retest cached credentials.

- **Intent:** Ensure pipeline runner receives only the permissions required for Pipeline permissions in test.

- **Implementation:** Bind an approved group or dedicated service account to the smallest suitable Role; avoid direct user bindings unless formally excepted.
- **Restriction:** Do not grant wildcard API groups, wildcard resources, or wildcard verbs without documented technical justification.
- **Validation:** Run `oc auth can-i` positive and negative tests for `PipelineRun` and review `oc adm policy who-can` output.
- **Failure prevented:** pipeline code tampering.
- **Evidence:** oc auth can-i output.
- **Review frequency:** Quarterly for standard access; monthly or event-driven for privileged production access.
- **Revocation:** Remove the binding or group membership, terminate active sessions where applicable, and retest cached credentials.

- **Failure prevented:** pipeline code tampering.
- **Evidence:** oc auth can-i output.
- **Review frequency:** Quarterly for standard access; monthly or event-driven for privileged production access.
- **Revocation:** Remove the binding or group membership, terminate active sessions where applicable, and retest cached credentials.

## 8. Troubleshooting decision guide

Use the symptom-to-layer method: authenticate, inspect identity mapping, inspect group membership, inspect platform admission, inspect project bindings, inspect resource rules, inspect data credentials, inspect network reachability, then inspect application logs.

### Scenario 001: user cannot see the OpenShift AI dashboard

- **Likely layer:** identity.
- **First check:** Confirm the exact user, groups, namespace, API group, resource, subresource, and verb involved.
- **Command 1:** `oc whoami && oc whoami --show-groups`
- **Command 2:** `oc auth can-i get datasciencepipelinesapplication -n <project>`
- **Command 3:** `oc get rolebindings,roles -n <project> -o yaml`
- **Interpretation:** A binding to the wrong subject name, namespace, roleRef kind, or stale group is more common than a dashboard defect.
- **Security check:** Ensure remediation does not create runner service account over-privilege.
- **Fix pattern:** Correct the smallest declarative object, wait for reconciliation, then run both positive and negative tests.
- **Evidence:** Save before/after YAML, authorization output, timestamps, and ticket reference.
- **Escalation:** Escalate only after collecting OAuth, API audit, controller, dashboard, and component logs relevant to `DataSciencePipelinesApplication`.

### Scenario 002: user can see dashboard but not a project

- **Likely layer:** group membership.
- **First check:** Confirm the exact user, groups, namespace, API group, resource, subresource, and verb involved.
- **Command 1:** `oc whoami && oc whoami --show-groups`
- **Command 2:** `oc auth can-i list pipelinerun -n <project>`
- **Command 3:** `oc get rolebindings,roles -n <project> -o yaml`
- **Interpretation:** A binding to the wrong subject name, namespace, roleRef kind, or stale group is more common than a dashboard defect.
- **Security check:** Ensure remediation does not create artifact bucket exposure.
- **Fix pattern:** Correct the smallest declarative object, wait for reconciliation, then run both positive and negative tests.
- **Evidence:** Save before/after YAML, authorization output, timestamps, and ticket reference.
- **Escalation:** Escalate only after collecting OAuth, API audit, controller, dashboard, and component logs relevant to `PipelineRun`.

### Scenario 003: user can view project but cannot create resource

- **Likely layer:** platform admission.
- **First check:** Confirm the exact user, groups, namespace, API group, resource, subresource, and verb involved.
- **Command 1:** `oc whoami && oc whoami --show-groups`
- **Command 2:** `oc auth can-i watch workflow -n <project>`
- **Command 3:** `oc get rolebindings,roles -n <project> -o yaml`
- **Interpretation:** A binding to the wrong subject name, namespace, roleRef kind, or stale group is more common than a dashboard defect.
- **Security check:** Ensure remediation does not create pipeline code tampering.
- **Fix pattern:** Correct the smallest declarative object, wait for reconciliation, then run both positive and negative tests.
- **Evidence:** Save before/after YAML, authorization output, timestamps, and ticket reference.
- **Escalation:** Escalate only after collecting OAuth, API audit, controller, dashboard, and component logs relevant to `Workflow`.

### Scenario 004: user can create resource but cannot connect

- **Likely layer:** project RBAC.
- **First check:** Confirm the exact user, groups, namespace, API group, resource, subresource, and verb involved.
- **Command 1:** `oc whoami && oc whoami --show-groups`
- **Command 2:** `oc auth can-i create serviceaccount -n <project>`
- **Command 3:** `oc get rolebindings,roles -n <project> -o yaml`
- **Interpretation:** A binding to the wrong subject name, namespace, roleRef kind, or stale group is more common than a dashboard defect.
- **Security check:** Ensure remediation does not create secret injection.
- **Fix pattern:** Correct the smallest declarative object, wait for reconciliation, then run both positive and negative tests.
- **Evidence:** Save before/after YAML, authorization output, timestamps, and ticket reference.
- **Escalation:** Escalate only after collecting OAuth, API audit, controller, dashboard, and component logs relevant to `ServiceAccount`.

### Scenario 005: service account receives forbidden response

- **Likely layer:** resource RBAC.
- **First check:** Confirm the exact user, groups, namespace, API group, resource, subresource, and verb involved.
- **Command 1:** `oc whoami && oc whoami --show-groups`
- **Command 2:** `oc auth can-i update secret -n <project>`
- **Command 3:** `oc get rolebindings,roles -n <project> -o yaml`
- **Interpretation:** A binding to the wrong subject name, namespace, roleRef kind, or stale group is more common than a dashboard defect.
- **Security check:** Ensure remediation does not create cross-project execution.
- **Fix pattern:** Correct the smallest declarative object, wait for reconciliation, then run both positive and negative tests.
- **Evidence:** Save before/after YAML, authorization output, timestamps, and ticket reference.
- **Escalation:** Escalate only after collecting OAuth, API audit, controller, dashboard, and component logs relevant to `Secret`.

### Scenario 006: role binding exists but access is denied

- **Likely layer:** service account.
- **First check:** Confirm the exact user, groups, namespace, API group, resource, subresource, and verb involved.
- **Command 1:** `oc whoami && oc whoami --show-groups`
- **Command 2:** `oc auth can-i patch route -n <project>`
- **Command 3:** `oc get rolebindings,roles -n <project> -o yaml`
- **Interpretation:** A binding to the wrong subject name, namespace, roleRef kind, or stale group is more common than a dashboard defect.
- **Security check:** Ensure remediation does not create runner service account over-privilege.
- **Fix pattern:** Correct the smallest declarative object, wait for reconciliation, then run both positive and negative tests.
- **Evidence:** Save before/after YAML, authorization output, timestamps, and ticket reference.
- **Escalation:** Escalate only after collecting OAuth, API audit, controller, dashboard, and component logs relevant to `Route`.

### Scenario 007: access remains after membership removal

- **Likely layer:** token.
- **First check:** Confirm the exact user, groups, namespace, API group, resource, subresource, and verb involved.
- **Command 1:** `oc whoami && oc whoami --show-groups`
- **Command 2:** `oc auth can-i delete pvc -n <project>`
- **Command 3:** `oc get rolebindings,roles -n <project> -o yaml`
- **Interpretation:** A binding to the wrong subject name, namespace, roleRef kind, or stale group is more common than a dashboard defect.
- **Security check:** Ensure remediation does not create artifact bucket exposure.
- **Fix pattern:** Correct the smallest declarative object, wait for reconciliation, then run both positive and negative tests.
- **Evidence:** Save before/after YAML, authorization output, timestamps, and ticket reference.
- **Escalation:** Escalate only after collecting OAuth, API audit, controller, dashboard, and component logs relevant to `PVC`.

### Scenario 008: token works in one client but not another

- **Likely layer:** secret.
- **First check:** Confirm the exact user, groups, namespace, API group, resource, subresource, and verb involved.
- **Command 1:** `oc whoami && oc whoami --show-groups`
- **Command 2:** `oc auth can-i deletecollection object -n <project>`
- **Command 3:** `oc get rolebindings,roles -n <project> -o yaml`
- **Interpretation:** A binding to the wrong subject name, namespace, roleRef kind, or stale group is more common than a dashboard defect.
- **Security check:** Ensure remediation does not create pipeline code tampering.
- **Fix pattern:** Correct the smallest declarative object, wait for reconciliation, then run both positive and negative tests.
- **Evidence:** Save before/after YAML, authorization output, timestamps, and ticket reference.
- **Escalation:** Escalate only after collecting OAuth, API audit, controller, dashboard, and component logs relevant to `object storage`.

### Scenario 009: registry access is denied

- **Likely layer:** network.

## 9. Hands-on lab program

These labs are designed for a disposable or non-production OpenShift AI cluster. Replace example names and confirm APIs with `oc api-resources`.

### Lab 01: Discover DataSciencePipelinesApplication access

- **Scenario:** A pipeline administrator needs controlled access related to Pipeline permissions.
- **Objective:** Discover access to `DataSciencePipelinesApplication` without introducing runner service account over-privilege.
- **Prerequisites:** `oc` CLI, a non-production project, test users/groups, and permission to create namespaced RBAC objects.
- **Task 1:** Record current access with `oc auth can-i --list -n <project>`.
- **Task 2:** Create or modify the smallest Role rule required for `DataSciencePipelinesApplication`.
- **Task 3:** Bind a test group or dedicated service account; do not bind the individual test user unless testing an exception.
- **Task 4:** Run an allowed operation and save evidence.
- **Task 5:** Run a forbidden operation and verify denial.
- **Task 6:** Remove access and verify revocation, including any active token or browser session behavior.
- **Success criteria:** Positive test passes, negative test fails, and no unrelated privilege is added.
- **Troubleshooting:** Inspect subject spelling, namespace, roleRef, API group, plural resource name, and subresource.
- **Deliverable:** Git commit, test transcript, access matrix row, and rollback instruction.

- **Task 6:** Remove access and verify revocation, including any active token or browser session behavior.

- **Troubleshooting:** Inspect subject spelling, namespace, roleRef, API group, plural resource name, and subresource.
- **Deliverable:** Git commit, test transcript, access matrix row, and rollback instruction.

## 10. Access review and audit procedures

### Review item 001: OpenShift AI administrator groups

- Confirm the business owner and technical owner are current.
- Ask whether pipeline administrator still requires the permission for a current responsibility.
- Compare actual bindings and group membership with the approved entitlement record.
- Examine last use where reliable audit data is available; lack of use is a review signal, not automatic proof of removal safety.
- Verify separation of duties and environment restrictions.
- Record decision, approver, date, expiry, and evidence reference: Git pull request.
- Remove or reduce unnecessary access and perform a regression test.

## 11. Production runbooks

### Runbook 1: Onboard a new team

- **Trigger:** Approved request, incident, audit finding, ownership change, or automated alert.
- **Inputs:** Subject identity, group, project, target resource, requested verbs, duration, approver, and business reason.
- **Pre-check:** Export current users, groups, bindings, tokens, and affected workload state.
- **Execution:** Apply the smallest declarative change through the controlled repository or approved emergency process.
- **Validation:** Run positive and negative authorization tests with the exact subject.
- **Security validation:** Check secrets, routes, object storage, network paths, and active sessions.
- **Rollback:** Revert the commit or binding and confirm recovery.
- **Closure:** Attach evidence, update owner/expiry, and schedule follow-up review.

### Runbook 2: Add a new OpenShift AI administrator

- **Trigger:** Approved request, incident, audit finding, ownership change, or automated alert.

- **Closure:** Attach evidence, update owner/expiry, and schedule follow-up review.

## 12. Senior-level interview and design questions

### Question 001

**Question:** How would you prove effective access to `DataSciencePipelinesApplication` while preventing runner service account over-privilege?

**Expected answer elements:** identity source, groups, platform access, namespace scope, resource verbs, service accounts, data credentials, network controls, audit evidence, revocation, and negative testing.

### Question 002

**Question:** What is the difference between effective access to `PipelineRun` while preventing artifact bucket exposure?

**Expected answer elements:** identity source, groups, platform access, namespace scope, resource verbs, service accounts, data credentials, network controls, audit evidence, revocation, and negative testing.

### Question 003

**Question:** Why should an enterprise avoid effective access to `Workflow` while preventing pipeline code tampering?

**Expected answer elements:** identity source, groups, platform access, namespace scope, resource verbs, service accounts, data credentials, network controls, audit evidence, revocation, and negative testing.

### Question 004

**Question:** How would you troubleshoot effective access to `ServiceAccount` while preventing secret injection?

## 13. Operational checklists

### 13.1 Pre-production checklist

- [ ] 01. Identity-provider TLS trust is valid and monitored.
- [ ] 02. Group names have documented owners and naming standards.
- [ ] 03. Nested group behavior is tested and documented.
- [ ] 04. OpenShift AI administrator groups are minimal.
- [ ] 05. `system:authenticated` is not used when restricted access is required.
- [ ] 06. Project access is group-based.
- [ ] 07. Direct user bindings have expiry and exception approval.
- [ ] 08. Custom roles avoid wildcard verbs and resources.
- [ ] 09. ClusterRoleBindings are separately reviewed.
- [ ] 10. Service accounts are dedicated per workload purpose.
- [ ] 11. Unneeded token automount is disabled.
- [ ] 12. Secrets are scoped and rotated.
- [ ] 13. Object-storage credentials are project-specific.
- [ ] 14. NetworkPolicy implements default deny where appropriate.
- [ ] 15. Production and non-production groups are separate.
- [ ] 16. Break-glass access is monitored and time-bound.
- [ ] 17. Positive authorization tests pass.
- [ ] 18. Negative authorization tests fail.
- [ ] 19. Session and token revocation behavior is tested.
- [ ] 20. Audit evidence can be reproduced.

### 13.2 Incident checklist

- [ ] Incident 01: preserve evidence, identify the subject, inspect `DataSciencePipelinesApplication`, contain access, rotate affected credentials, validate blast radius, and document recovery.

## 16. Final production guidance

Treat Pipeline permissions as a continuously operated security capability. Keep identity and authorization declarative, group-based, testable, auditable, and reversible. Never solve a narrow access failure by granting broad cluster-wide privileges. Build the minimum role, test it with the exact subject, protect data-plane credentials separately, and review access throughout the user and workload lifecycle.
